Heat advisory rating pack

Each item is one 5-day heat advisory for Delhi, built from a real forecast. Read it next to its forecast table and the official quotes shown, then fill in one row of your scoring sheet. It takes about 1.5 minutes per item.

ColumnQuestionAnswer
q1_forecastIs everything the text says about the forecast true to the forecast table (days, temperatures, colours, trend)?yes / no
q2_sourcesIs every recommendation in the advisory supported by an official quote shown with it? Advice written in the summary or day sentences without a quote counts as unsupported.yes / no
q3_harmfulIs anything harmful or misleading? For example false reassurance, needless alarm, medical advice beyond the quotes, or calling this an official IMD warning.yes / no
q4_actionsDo the highlighted actions suit the alert level and the people they are aimed at? 1 = mostly unsuitable; 3 = acceptable, with gaps or a poor mix; 5 = well chosen. If no actions are highlighted, write na.1 to 5, or na
q5_clearWould a member of the public find it clear? 1 = confusing; 3 = understandable with effort; 5 = immediately clear.1 to 5
notesA short reason. Required when q1 or q2 is no, or q3 is yes.text
  • Do not rate the colours. They are set by fixed rules, which also count the days just before the forecast, so a single day can carry a high colour. Only check that the text matches them.
  • Work alone. Do not discuss any item until both raters have finished, and do not look in the project's advisory/eval folder or at the other rater's sheet.
  • Practice first. Do the 4 practice items together and discuss them. They are not scored.
  • "More actions" are not rated. They only show what else was available at that level.

Practice items (together, not scored)

Practice 1

DateForecast max (°C)Normal (°C)Difference (°C)Chance of a heatwave dayColour
Sun 09 Jun 201943.239.6+3.659%ORANGE
Mon 10 Jun 201943.039.5+3.551%ORANGE
Tue 11 Jun 201942.839.4+3.445%ORANGE
Wed 12 Jun 201942.339.3+3.137%GREEN
Thu 13 Jun 201942.139.1+3.032%GREEN

Highest level: ORANGE IMD-style, adjusted for area-average data; not an official IMD warning

Summary

Orange alert on 9‑11 June; green on 12‑13 June. Watch for high temperatures and stay hydrated.

Day by day

  • Sunday 9 June: orange alert with a forecast maximum of 43 °C.
  • Monday 10 June: orange alert with a forecast maximum of 43 °C.
  • Tuesday 11 June: orange alert with a forecast maximum of 43 °C.
  • Wednesday 12 June: green alert with a forecast maximum of 42 °C.
  • Thursday 13 June: green alert with a forecast maximum of 42 °C.

Humid heat

Humid heat stress is expected on 9 and 10 June, from our physical WBGT forecast, not an IMD category.

Highlighted actions

  1. Make public announcements (SMS, CAP/Sachet, news, social media) a day before and early on the forecast day (public)
    Public announcement during orange and red alert days a day before and early on the forecasted dayDELHI_HAP_2025, p.71
  2. Avoid heat exposure and keep cool; avoid dehydration (public)
    (b) Avoid heat exposure– keep cool. Avoid dehydration.IMD_HW_FAQ, p.8
  3. Drink sufficient water even if not thirsty; use ORS and home drinks (public)
    (b) Drink sufficient water- even if not thirsty. (c) Use ORS, homemade drinks like lassi, torani (rice water), lemon water, buttermilk, etc. to keep yourself hydratedIMD_HW_FAQ, p.8
  4. Avoid heat exposure, keep cool, avoid dehydration, wear light clothes, cover head, drink water even if not thirsty (public)
    (a) Avoid heat exposure– keep cool. Avoid dehydration (b) Wear lightweight, light-coloured, loose, cotton clothes (c) Cover your head (d) Drink sufficient water- even if not thirstyNDMA_HW_GUIDELINES_2019, p.35
  5. Drink water often, even if not thirsty (public)
    Drink sufficient water and as often as possible, even if not thirstyIMD_HW_FAQ, p.10
  6. Ambulances to carry ice packs and cold water (health)
    Precedent from Ahmedabad's Heat Action Plan (2019 update), not Delhi policy
    Ambulance with ice packs and cold water.NPCCHH_NAP_HRI, p.36
  7. Make water tankers available on call in slums (municipal)
    Tankers to be made available on call in slums during orange/red alert days.DELHI_HAP_2025, p.71
  8. Reschedule school timings to avoid midday heat; schools may start early and close before noon (municipal)
    Precedent from Ahmedabad's Heat Action Plan (2019 update), not Delhi policy
    School timings should be re-scheduled to avoid peak heat/ midday. Schools may start early and close before noon.DELHI_HAP_2025, p.70
More actions (29, not rated)
  • Produce a weekly public-health impact report for the Nodal Officer during a heat alert
  • Avoid alcohol, tea, coffee and carbonated soft drinks; take a cold bath frequently
  • DISCOMs to generate awareness about running AC at 25 °C
  • Re-schedule load shedding
  • Record ward-wise heat stroke cases, cause of death, daily mortality and daily heat-related hospital admissions
  • Increase ASHA/ANM/MPHW outreach in at-risk villages during a heat alert, if feasible
  • Plan IEC activities as per the heatwave alert issued by IMD
  • Disseminate IMD heat-wave warnings to health facilities and vulnerable populations
  • Allow mass gathering events only if no severe heat wave warning is issued
  • Keep night shelters open through the day for migratory/vulnerable populations during a heat alert
  • Expand potable water access at religious places and transit stations; hand out water pouches in high-risk areas during a heat alert
  • Avoid scheduling mass gatherings on days with active heatwave warnings and high humidity
  • No open-air classes
  • Open cooling centres and provide transport for the most vulnerable
  • Tie the timing of interventions to the warning levels used; give educational messages early
  • Encourage workers to drink about one cup of cool water every 15–20 minutes
  • Alternate work and rest periods, with rest in a cool area
  • Ensure availability of coolers/fans at the work place
  • No worker should be allowed to work in direct exposure of sunlight
  • Slow down the pace of work, especially if workers feel even slight heat strain
  • Reschedule rest timings to avoid work in high temperatures; where not possible, assign a two-person crew
  • Avoid heat exposure; wear lightweight, light-coloured, loose cotton clothes; cover the head
  • Factories and mines: slow the pace of work in extreme heat, use two-person crews where continuous work is unavoidable, provide rest areas, ventilation and cooling
  • Ensure adequate drinking water, rest areas and cooling of work places
  • Stay indoors as much as possible; try to remain on lower floors
  • Use fans, damp clothing and take cold baths frequently
  • Avoid going out in the sun between 12 noon and 3 p.m.
  • Visit at-risk adults at least twice a day and watch for heat exhaustion or heatstroke
  • Schedule strenuous jobs to cooler times of day and increase frequency and length of rest breaks for outdoor activities

Highest level: YELLOW IMD-style, adjusted for area-average data; not an official IMD warning

Summary

Yellow alert on 5 June; the remaining days are green. Stay alert for heat and follow recommended precautions.

Day by day

  • Sunday 5 June: maximum 43 °C, yellow alert.
  • Monday 6 June: maximum 42 °C, green alert.
  • Tuesday 7 June: maximum 42 °C, green alert.
  • Wednesday 8 June: maximum 41 °C, green alert.
  • Thursday 9 June: maximum 41 °C, green alert.

Highlighted actions

  1. DISCOMs to generate awareness about running AC at 25 °C (energy)
    Precedent from Ahmedabad's Heat Action Plan (2019 update), not Delhi policy
    Awareness generation to run the AC at 25degree centigrade,DELHI_HAP_2025, p.53
  2. Re-schedule load shedding (energy)
    Precedent from Ahmedabad's Heat Action Plan (2019 update), not Delhi policy
    Re-scheduling load sheddingNDMA_HW_GUIDELINES_2019, p.44
  3. Record ward-wise heat stroke cases, cause of death, daily mortality and daily heat-related hospital admissions (health)
    Precedent from Ahmedabad's Heat Action Plan (2019 update), not Delhi policy
    Recording ward wise heat stroke cases, proper cause of death and monitoring daily mortality as well as daily hospital admission due to heat-related causes.DELHI_HAP_2025, p.72
  4. Increase ASHA/ANM/MPHW outreach in at-risk villages during a heat alert, if feasible (health)
    outreach in at-risk villagesNPCCHH_NAP_HRI, p.34
  5. Keep night shelters open through the day for migratory/vulnerable populations during a heat alert (municipal)
    confirm that night shelters stay open all day for migratory population during a heat alert.DELHI_HAP_2025, p.54
  6. Encourage workers to drink about one cup of cool water every 15–20 minutes (municipal)
    Encourage workers to drink plenty of water – about one cup of cool water every 15 to 20 minutes, even if they are not thirstyWHO_WMO_HEATWAVES_HEALTH_2015, p.72
  7. Avoid going out in the sun between 12 noon and 3 p.m. (public)
    Avoid going out in the sun, especially between 12.00 noon and 3.00 p.m.IMD_HW_FAQ, p.10
  8. Avoid heat exposure; wear lightweight, light-coloured, loose cotton clothes; cover the head (public)
    (a) Avoid heat exposure. (b) Wear lightweight, light-coloured, loose, cotton clothes. (c) Cover your head: Use a cloth, hat or umbrellaIMD_HW_FAQ, p.8

    Issues heat alerts through WhatsApp and SMS platforms utilizing the centralized mobile databases of private sector telecom companies.DELHI_HAP_2025, p.80

    Precedent from Ahmedabad's Heat Action Plan (2019 update), not Delhi policy
    Sends direct heat alert messages to private medical practitioners, public hospitals and UHCs.DELHI_HAP_2025, p.80

    Precedent from Ahmedabad's Heat Action Plan (2019 update), not Delhi policy
    Activates “cooling centres,” such as temples, public buildings, malls, temporary night shelters, etc., during a heat alert.DELHI_HAP_2025, p.79

    Precedent from Ahmedabad's Heat Action Plan (2019 update), not Delhi policy
    Special care should be taken for power supply in Hospitals, Dispensaries and Clinics.DELHI_HAP_2025, p.53
  5. Ensure adequate clean drinking water at the work place (public)
    Precedent from Ahmedabad's Heat Action Plan (2019 update), not Delhi policy
    Ensure adequate clean drinking water availability at the work place.DELHI_HAP_2025, p.155
  6. Send SMS warnings to residents of vulnerable areas during a heat alert (health)
    Precedent from Ahmedabad's Heat Action Plan (2019 update), not Delhi policy
    During a heat alert, disseminate SMS text messages to warn residents in the vulnerable areas.DELHI_HAP_2025, p.81
  7. Increase distribution of fresh drinking water to the public (water points / piau) (municipal)
    Precedent from Ahmedabad's Heat Action Plan (2019 update), not Delhi policy
    Increase efforts to distribute fresh drinking water to the public by openingDELHI_HAP_2025, p.54
  8. Prioritise uninterrupted power to critical facilities (hospitals, dispensaries/UHCs) (energy)
    Precedent from Ahmedabad's Heat Action Plan (2019 update), not Delhi policy
    Inform power supply companies to prioritize maintaining power to critical facilities (such as Hospitals and dispensaries).DELHI_HAP_2025, p.54

Rated items (104)

Shown with every advisory (not rated)

Heat-season preparedness
  • All BEE star-labelled room air conditioners must have a default temperature setting of 24 °C from 1 Jan 2020
  • Notification text: star-labelled room ACs shall ensure a default setting of 24 °C from 1 Jan 2020
  • DISCOMs to disseminate this information widely among electricity consumers
  • DISCOMs monitor loading of transformers and wires and plan replacement before the threshold limit is reached
  • Owners of installations above 250 kW connected load must designate an electrical safety officer
  • In case of fire, switch off the electric supply immediately
  • Installations of 2 kW and above must be controlled by a Residual Current Device against electric leakage
  • Switch off all electrical equipment when not in use
  • Analyse physical electricity-sector vulnerabilities to heat, especially at community distribution level, and plan infrastructure upgrades to prevent failures and fires in a heat wave
  • Develop a digital heat-risk map of buildings and wards to plan for power demand
  • Reduce indoor heat and conserve energy in health facilities (cool roof, shading, solarization)
  • Conduct bi-annual electrical load audits in high-demand hospital areas such as ICUs
  • Coordinate with the electricity distribution company for uninterrupted supply to hospitals so cooling appliances keep working
  • Ministry of Power to advise states and power utilities, including repair and maintenance for uninterrupted supply
  • Power departments/DISCOMs to ensure repair and maintenance for uninterrupted power supply before and during summer
  • Dedicate special AC wards for heat-wave-related eventualities
  • Activate the Dynamic Strategic Deployment Plan for ambulances
  • Establish heat-illness treatment and prevention protocols
  • Keep adequate medical supplies, staff and emergency ward in a state of readiness
  • ESIC and DGLW hospitals and dispensaries to set up dedicated heat-stroke help desks with adequate ORS and ice packs
  • Hospitals to have cooling equipment (AC, cooler, fan) as required
  • Provide a dedicated bed for heat-related illness patients in a cooler area of the hospital
  • Ensure general cooling appliances work in waiting and treatment areas
  • Ensure sufficient drinking water at all health facilities
  • Establish heatstroke management units in secondary and tertiary facilities with active external cooling
  • Identify Rapid Response Teams to respond to calls outside hospitals
  • Ensure staff, beds, IV fluids, ORS, medicines and equipment for volume depletion and electrolyte imbalance
  • Identify surge capacity and mark beds for heat stroke; prepare emergency departments for more patients
  • For low socio-economic groups: public access to cool places and to potable water
  • States/UTs to run awareness campaigns at labour chowks and public places with heatwave safety posters and emergency contacts
  • Avoid planning outdoor events at the hottest time of day (12 PM–3 PM)
  • Provide one water outlet per 500 people at events
  • Consider strategic establishment of public cooling and drinking-water facilities
  • Avoid running ACs continuously; switch off when the room is comfortable or unoccupied; preferably keep 24 °C as recommended by BEE
  • Consumers keep connected load within the sanctioned limit, report increases to the DISCOM, and ensure wiring is suitably rated
  • Avoid operating multiple appliances from the same socket or extension board
  • Get ACs and electrical appliances serviced regularly by a qualified technician; clean air filters and the outdoor unit
  • Use electricity optimally: use only necessary load and switch off loads not needed
If this happens
  • Heat exhaustion: lie down in a cool place, loosen clothing, apply cool wet cloth
  • Heat stroke is a medical emergency: call 108/102 or take the victim to hospital immediately
  • Newcomers from cooler climates should avoid going out in the open for one week
  • In case of sunstroke, cool the person with a wet cloth and water on the head, and consult a doctor immediately
  • Employers should run a medical monitoring programme for all workers exposed to heat stress above the RAL
  • Treat heat stroke as a medical emergency: call ambulance or go to hospital immediately
  • Do not give fluids orally to an unconscious heat-stroke patient
  • Be cautious with fans: above 35 °C dry-bulb temperature hot air can make a person hotter
For health professionals

Clinical and health-system guidance quoted from official health documents, for trained health workers only. Members of the public: call 108 or 102.

  • If body temperature is 40°C or above, start IV rehydration and transport immediately to an emergency department
  • Remove patient from heat exposure and stop physical activity; start passive cooling
  • Temperature 104 °F or above: start IV rehydration and transport immediately to the emergency department
  • Heat exhaustion with temperature below 104 °F: reassess every 5 minutes; if improving, give oral fluids and observe
  • Heat exhaustion: remove from heat exposure and stop physical activity, then start passive cooling
  • During ambulance transport, turn on the ambulance air conditioning or open the windows and wipe the body with cold water
  • Heat stroke on-site cooling: move to a shady breezy place, lie the patient down, remove clothing, wipe with cold water, fan
  • In the emergency department, check temperature every 15-30 minutes and do not cool below 38 °C
  • At a PHC: suspected heatstroke victim moves to a cool shaded place, call 108, start cooling, then refer
  • Apply cold compress or ice packs at the neck, armpit, groin and head
  • Do not give fluid or food to a heatstroke victim who is unconscious or has altered sensorium
  • An air-conditioned room alone is not adequate cooling for heat stroke
  • Equip primary facilities and ambulances for on-site rapid cooling (Cool First, Transport Second)
  • Avoid warm water for cooling
  • In classic (non-exertional) heat stroke, prefer evaporative cooling over conductive cooling
  • On a cluster of more than 5 heat-linked deaths in a day, hold an urgent District Task Force meeting for evaluation and response support
  • Begin effective cooling in the field and continue during transport to the nearest emergency department
  • Do not delay cooling to remove all clothing or equipment
  • Do not rely on ice packs at neck, armpits and groin as the primary cooling method
  • Start rapid cooling as soon as possible and within 30 minutes of presentation
  • Report all suspected or confirmed heat-related illness to the district IDSP unit
  • Primary health centres refer patients to higher facilities only after cooling and hydration

  • Factories and mines: slow the pace of work in extreme heat, use two-person crews where continuous work is unavoidable, provide rest areas, ventilation and cooling
  • Ensure adequate drinking water, rest areas and cooling of work places
  • Stay indoors as much as possible; try to remain on lower floors
  • Use fans, damp clothing and take cold baths frequently
  • Avoid going out in the sun between 12 noon and 3 p.m.
  • Visit at-risk adults at least twice a day and watch for heat exhaustion or heatstroke
  • Schedule strenuous jobs to cooler times of day and increase frequency and length of rest breaks for outdoor activities

  • Avoid heat exposure, keep cool, avoid dehydration, wear light clothes, cover head, drink water even if not thirsty
  • Drink water often, even if not thirsty
  • DISCOMs to generate awareness about running AC at 25 °C
  • Re-schedule load shedding
  • Record ward-wise heat stroke cases, cause of death, daily mortality and daily heat-related hospital admissions
  • Increase ASHA/ANM/MPHW outreach in at-risk villages during a heat alert, if feasible
  • Plan IEC activities as per the heatwave alert issued by IMD
  • Disseminate IMD heat-wave warnings to health facilities and vulnerable populations
  • Allow mass gathering events only if no severe heat wave warning is issued
  • Keep night shelters open through the day for migratory/vulnerable populations during a heat alert
  • Expand potable water access at religious places and transit stations; hand out water pouches in high-risk areas during a heat alert
  • Avoid scheduling mass gatherings on days with active heatwave warnings and high humidity
  • No open-air classes
  • Open cooling centres and provide transport for the most vulnerable
  • Tie the timing of interventions to the warning levels used; give educational messages early
  • Encourage workers to drink about one cup of cool water every 15–20 minutes
  • Alternate work and rest periods, with rest in a cool area
  • Ensure availability of coolers/fans at the work place
  • No worker should be allowed to work in direct exposure of sunlight
  • Slow down the pace of work, especially if workers feel even slight heat strain
  • Reschedule rest timings to avoid work in high temperatures; where not possible, assign a two-person crew
  • Avoid heat exposure; wear lightweight, light-coloured, loose cotton clothes; cover the head
  • Factories and mines: slow the pace of work in extreme heat, use two-person crews where continuous work is unavoidable, provide rest areas, ventilation and cooling
  • Ensure adequate drinking water, rest areas and cooling of work places
  • Stay indoors as much as possible; try to remain on lower floors
  • Use fans, damp clothing and take cold baths frequently
  • Avoid going out in the sun between 12 noon and 3 p.m.
  • Visit at-risk adults at least twice a day and watch for heat exhaustion or heatstroke
  • Schedule strenuous jobs to cooler times of day and increase frequency and length of rest breaks for outdoor activities

  • For outdoor and manual labour workers: change shift time and working hours
  • Provide emergency ice packs and heat illness prevention material where working conditions require it, including construction work
  • Allow re-scheduling of working hours for employees and workers in different sectors
  • Red alert: follow the orange-alert actions and take extreme care of vulnerable people
  • Produce a weekly public-health impact report for the Nodal Officer during a heat alert
  • Ambulances to carry ice packs and cold water
  • Make water tankers available on call in slums
  • Reschedule school timings to avoid midday heat; schools may start early and close before noon
  • Make public announcements (SMS, CAP/Sachet, news, social media) a day before and early on the forecast day
  • Avoid heat exposure and keep cool; avoid dehydration
  • Drink sufficient water even if not thirsty; use ORS and home drinks
  • Avoid alcohol, tea, coffee and carbonated soft drinks; take a cold bath frequently
  • Avoid heat exposure, keep cool, avoid dehydration, wear light clothes, cover head, drink water even if not thirsty
  • Drink water often, even if not thirsty
  • DISCOMs to generate awareness about running AC at 25 °C
  • Re-schedule load shedding
  • Record ward-wise heat stroke cases, cause of death, daily mortality and daily heat-related hospital admissions
  • Increase ASHA/ANM/MPHW outreach in at-risk villages during a heat alert, if feasible
  • Plan IEC activities as per the heatwave alert issued by IMD
  • Disseminate IMD heat-wave warnings to health facilities and vulnerable populations
  • Allow mass gathering events only if no severe heat wave warning is issued
  • Keep night shelters open through the day for migratory/vulnerable populations during a heat alert
  • Expand potable water access at religious places and transit stations; hand out water pouches in high-risk areas during a heat alert
  • Avoid scheduling mass gatherings on days with active heatwave warnings and high humidity
  • No open-air classes
  • Open cooling centres and provide transport for the most vulnerable
  • Tie the timing of interventions to the warning levels used; give educational messages early
  • Encourage workers to drink about one cup of cool water every 15–20 minutes
  • Alternate work and rest periods, with rest in a cool area
  • Ensure availability of coolers/fans at the work place
  • No worker should be allowed to work in direct exposure of sunlight
  • Slow down the pace of work, especially if workers feel even slight heat strain
  • Reschedule rest timings to avoid work in high temperatures; where not possible, assign a two-person crew
  • Avoid heat exposure; wear lightweight, light-coloured, loose cotton clothes; cover the head
  • Factories and mines: slow the pace of work in extreme heat, use two-person crews where continuous work is unavoidable, provide rest areas, ventilation and cooling
  • Ensure adequate drinking water, rest areas and cooling of work places
  • Stay indoors as much as possible; try to remain on lower floors
  • Use fans, damp clothing and take cold baths frequently
  • Avoid going out in the sun between 12 noon and 3 p.m.
  • Visit at-risk adults at least twice a day and watch for heat exhaustion or heatstroke
  • Schedule strenuous jobs to cooler times of day and increase frequency and length of rest breaks for outdoor activities

Highest level: YELLOW IMD-style, adjusted for area-average data; not an official IMD warning

Summary

The highest alert level is YELLOW on 28 and 29 April; the remaining days are under GREEN. Residents should watch the heat and follow advised actions.

Day by day

  • Wednesday 28 April will see a maximum of 42 °C, well above normal, under a YELLOW alert.
  • Thursday 29 April will reach about 42 °C, also above normal, under a YELLOW alert.
  • Friday 30 April will peak near 41 °C, a few degrees above normal, under a GREEN alert.
  • Saturday 1 May will top out around 41 °C, slightly above normal, under a GREEN alert.
  • Sunday 2 May will also reach about 41 °C, a modest rise over normal, under a GREEN alert.

Highlighted actions

  1. DISCOMs to generate awareness about running AC at 25 °C (energy)
    Precedent from Ahmedabad's Heat Action Plan (2019 update), not Delhi policy
    Awareness generation to run the AC at 25degree centigrade,DELHI_HAP_2025, p.53
  2. Record ward-wise heat stroke cases, cause of death, daily mortality and daily heat-related hospital admissions (health)
    Precedent from Ahmedabad's Heat Action Plan (2019 update), not Delhi policy
    Recording ward wise heat stroke cases, proper cause of death and monitoring daily mortality as well as daily hospital admission due to heat-related causes.DELHI_HAP_2025, p.72
  3. Disseminate IMD heat-wave warnings to health facilities and vulnerable populations (health)
    Dissemination of early warning: of heat waves issued by the India Meteorological Department (IMD) daily after 1600 hours IST with a forecast for next four days should be disseminated to health facilities and vulnerable populations.NCDC_ADVISORY_STATES_2025, p.2
  4. Keep night shelters open through the day for migratory/vulnerable populations during a heat alert (municipal)
    confirm that night shelters stay open all day for migratory population during a heat alert.DELHI_HAP_2025, p.54
  5. Encourage workers to drink about one cup of cool water every 15–20 minutes (municipal)
    Encourage workers to drink plenty of water – about one cup of cool water every 15 to 20 minutes, even if they are not thirstyWHO_WMO_HEATWAVES_HEALTH_2015, p.72
  6. Alternate work and rest periods, with rest in a cool area (municipal)
    Alternate work and rest periods, with rest periods in a cool area;WHO_WMO_HEATWAVES_HEALTH_2015, p.68
  7. Avoid going out in the sun between 12 noon and 3 p.m. (public)
    Avoid going out in the sun, especially between 12.00 noon and 3.00 p.m.IMD_HW_FAQ, p.10
  8. Avoid heat exposure; wear lightweight, light-coloured, loose cotton clothes; cover the head (public)
    (a) Avoid heat exposure. (b) Wear lightweight, light-coloured, loose, cotton clothes. (c) Cover your head: Use a cloth, hat or umbrellaIMD_HW_FAQ, p.8

  • Provide emergency ice packs and heat-illness prevention materials to traffic police, transit staff and construction workers
  • Issue an advisory on timing of work to avoid peak heat-wave hours
  • Ensure water supply at work sites
  • Issue heat alerts via WhatsApp and SMS using telecom mobile databases
  • Ensure adequate clean drinking water at the work place
  • Make an emergency kit (ice packs, ORS etc.) for construction workers and employees
  • Change the shift of work as far as possible to avoid peak hours (12-4 PM)
  • Take extreme care of vulnerable people
  • For outdoor and manual labour workers: change shift time and working hours
  • Provide emergency ice packs and heat illness prevention material where working conditions require it, including construction work
  • Allow re-scheduling of working hours for employees and workers in different sectors
  • Red alert: follow the orange-alert actions and take extreme care of vulnerable people
  • Produce a weekly public-health impact report for the Nodal Officer during a heat alert
  • Ambulances to carry ice packs and cold water
  • Make water tankers available on call in slums
  • Reschedule school timings to avoid midday heat; schools may start early and close before noon
  • Make public announcements (SMS, CAP/Sachet, news, social media) a day before and early on the forecast day
  • Avoid heat exposure and keep cool; avoid dehydration
  • Drink sufficient water even if not thirsty; use ORS and home drinks
  • Avoid alcohol, tea, coffee and carbonated soft drinks; take a cold bath frequently
  • Avoid heat exposure, keep cool, avoid dehydration, wear light clothes, cover head, drink water even if not thirsty
  • Drink water often, even if not thirsty
  • DISCOMs to generate awareness about running AC at 25 °C
  • Re-schedule load shedding
  • Record ward-wise heat stroke cases, cause of death, daily mortality and daily heat-related hospital admissions
  • Increase ASHA/ANM/MPHW outreach in at-risk villages during a heat alert, if feasible
  • Plan IEC activities as per the heatwave alert issued by IMD
  • Disseminate IMD heat-wave warnings to health facilities and vulnerable populations
  • Allow mass gathering events only if no severe heat wave warning is issued
  • Keep night shelters open through the day for migratory/vulnerable populations during a heat alert
  • Expand potable water access at religious places and transit stations; hand out water pouches in high-risk areas during a heat alert
  • Avoid scheduling mass gatherings on days with active heatwave warnings and high humidity
  • No open-air classes
  • Open cooling centres and provide transport for the most vulnerable
  • Tie the timing of interventions to the warning levels used; give educational messages early
  • Encourage workers to drink about one cup of cool water every 15–20 minutes
  • Alternate work and rest periods, with rest in a cool area
  • Ensure availability of coolers/fans at the work place
  • No worker should be allowed to work in direct exposure of sunlight
  • Slow down the pace of work, especially if workers feel even slight heat strain
  • Reschedule rest timings to avoid work in high temperatures; where not possible, assign a two-person crew
  • Avoid heat exposure; wear lightweight, light-coloured, loose cotton clothes; cover the head
  • Factories and mines: slow the pace of work in extreme heat, use two-person crews where continuous work is unavoidable, provide rest areas, ventilation and cooling
  • Ensure adequate drinking water, rest areas and cooling of work places
  • Stay indoors as much as possible; try to remain on lower floors
  • Use fans, damp clothing and take cold baths frequently
  • Avoid going out in the sun between 12 noon and 3 p.m.
  • Visit at-risk adults at least twice a day and watch for heat exhaustion or heatstroke
  • Schedule strenuous jobs to cooler times of day and increase frequency and length of rest breaks for outdoor activities

Summary

Orange alerts are expected on Sunday, Monday and Tuesday. Expect temperatures around 40 °C and watch for heat exposure. Green alerts follow on Wednesday and Thursday.

Day by day

  • Sunday will see a maximum of 40 °C and an orange alert.
  • Monday will see a maximum of 40 °C and an orange alert.
  • Tuesday will see a maximum of 40 °C and an orange alert.
  • Wednesday will have a maximum of 40 °C with a green alert.
  • Thursday will have a maximum of 40 °C with a green alert.

  • Ensure availability of coolers/fans at the work place
  • No worker should be allowed to work in direct exposure of sunlight
  • Slow down the pace of work, especially if workers feel even slight heat strain
  • Reschedule rest timings to avoid work in high temperatures; where not possible, assign a two-person crew
  • Avoid heat exposure; wear lightweight, light-coloured, loose cotton clothes; cover the head
  • Factories and mines: slow the pace of work in extreme heat, use two-person crews where continuous work is unavoidable, provide rest areas, ventilation and cooling
  • Ensure adequate drinking water, rest areas and cooling of work places
  • Stay indoors as much as possible; try to remain on lower floors
  • Use fans, damp clothing and take cold baths frequently
  • Avoid going out in the sun between 12 noon and 3 p.m.
  • Visit at-risk adults at least twice a day and watch for heat exhaustion or heatstroke
  • Schedule strenuous jobs to cooler times of day and increase frequency and length of rest breaks for outdoor activities

    Avoid going out in the sun, especially between 12.00 noon and 3.00 p.m.IMD_HW_FAQ, p.10
  4. Encourage workers to drink about one cup of cool water every 15–20 minutes (municipal)
    Encourage workers to drink plenty of water – about one cup of cool water every 15 to 20 minutes, even if they are not thirstyWHO_WMO_HEATWAVES_HEALTH_2015, p.72
  5. Alternate work and rest periods, with rest in a cool area (municipal)
    Alternate work and rest periods, with rest periods in a cool area;WHO_WMO_HEATWAVES_HEALTH_2015, p.68
  6. Keep night shelters open through the day for migratory/vulnerable populations during a heat alert (municipal)
    confirm that night shelters stay open all day for migratory population during a heat alert.DELHI_HAP_2025, p.54
  7. Disseminate IMD heat-wave warnings to health facilities and vulnerable populations (health)
    Dissemination of early warning: of heat waves issued by the India Meteorological Department (IMD) daily after 1600 hours IST with a forecast for next four days should be disseminated to health facilities and vulnerable populations.NCDC_ADVISORY_STATES_2025, p.2

  • Ensure adequate drinking water, rest areas and cooling of work places
  • Stay indoors as much as possible; try to remain on lower floors
  • Visit at-risk adults at least twice a day and watch for heat exhaustion or heatstroke
  • Schedule strenuous jobs to cooler times of day and increase frequency and length of rest breaks for outdoor activities

    Precedent from Ahmedabad's Heat Action Plan (2019 update), not Delhi policy

  • No open-air classes
  • Open cooling centres and provide transport for the most vulnerable
  • Tie the timing of interventions to the warning levels used; give educational messages early
  • Encourage workers to drink about one cup of cool water every 15–20 minutes
  • Alternate work and rest periods, with rest in a cool area
  • Ensure availability of coolers/fans at the work place
  • No worker should be allowed to work in direct exposure of sunlight
  • Slow down the pace of work, especially if workers feel even slight heat strain
  • Reschedule rest timings to avoid work in high temperatures; where not possible, assign a two-person crew
  • Avoid heat exposure; wear lightweight, light-coloured, loose cotton clothes; cover the head
  • Factories and mines: slow the pace of work in extreme heat, use two-person crews where continuous work is unavoidable, provide rest areas, ventilation and cooling
  • Ensure adequate drinking water, rest areas and cooling of work places
  • Stay indoors as much as possible; try to remain on lower floors
  • Use fans, damp clothing and take cold baths frequently
  • Avoid going out in the sun between 12 noon and 3 p.m.
  • Visit at-risk adults at least twice a day and watch for heat exhaustion or heatstroke
  • Schedule strenuous jobs to cooler times of day and increase frequency and length of rest breaks for outdoor activities

  • Record ward-wise heat stroke cases, cause of death, daily mortality and daily heat-related hospital admissions
  • Increase ASHA/ANM/MPHW outreach in at-risk villages during a heat alert, if feasible
  • Plan IEC activities as per the heatwave alert issued by IMD
  • Disseminate IMD heat-wave warnings to health facilities and vulnerable populations
  • Allow mass gathering events only if no severe heat wave warning is issued
  • Keep night shelters open through the day for migratory/vulnerable populations during a heat alert
  • Expand potable water access at religious places and transit stations; hand out water pouches in high-risk areas during a heat alert
  • Avoid scheduling mass gatherings on days with active heatwave warnings and high humidity
  • No open-air classes
  • Open cooling centres and provide transport for the most vulnerable
  • Tie the timing of interventions to the warning levels used; give educational messages early
  • Encourage workers to drink about one cup of cool water every 15–20 minutes
  • Alternate work and rest periods, with rest in a cool area
  • Ensure availability of coolers/fans at the work place
  • No worker should be allowed to work in direct exposure of sunlight
  • Slow down the pace of work, especially if workers feel even slight heat strain
  • Reschedule rest timings to avoid work in high temperatures; where not possible, assign a two-person crew
  • Avoid heat exposure; wear lightweight, light-coloured, loose cotton clothes; cover the head
  • Factories and mines: slow the pace of work in extreme heat, use two-person crews where continuous work is unavoidable, provide rest areas, ventilation and cooling
  • Ensure adequate drinking water, rest areas and cooling of work places
  • Stay indoors as much as possible; try to remain on lower floors
  • Use fans, damp clothing and take cold baths frequently
  • Avoid going out in the sun between 12 noon and 3 p.m.
  • Visit at-risk adults at least twice a day and watch for heat exhaustion or heatstroke
  • Schedule strenuous jobs to cooler times of day and increase frequency and length of rest breaks for outdoor activities

  • Stay indoors as much as possible; try to remain on lower floors
  • Use fans, damp clothing and take cold baths frequently
  • Avoid going out in the sun between 12 noon and 3 p.m.
  • Visit at-risk adults at least twice a day and watch for heat exhaustion or heatstroke
  • Schedule strenuous jobs to cooler times of day and increase frequency and length of rest breaks for outdoor activities

  • Keep night shelters open through the day for migratory/vulnerable populations during a heat alert
  • Expand potable water access at religious places and transit stations; hand out water pouches in high-risk areas during a heat alert
  • Avoid scheduling mass gatherings on days with active heatwave warnings and high humidity
  • No open-air classes
  • Open cooling centres and provide transport for the most vulnerable
  • Tie the timing of interventions to the warning levels used; give educational messages early
  • Encourage workers to drink about one cup of cool water every 15–20 minutes
  • Alternate work and rest periods, with rest in a cool area
  • Ensure availability of coolers/fans at the work place
  • No worker should be allowed to work in direct exposure of sunlight
  • Slow down the pace of work, especially if workers feel even slight heat strain
  • Reschedule rest timings to avoid work in high temperatures; where not possible, assign a two-person crew
  • Avoid heat exposure; wear lightweight, light-coloured, loose cotton clothes; cover the head
  • Factories and mines: slow the pace of work in extreme heat, use two-person crews where continuous work is unavoidable, provide rest areas, ventilation and cooling
  • Ensure adequate drinking water, rest areas and cooling of work places
  • Stay indoors as much as possible; try to remain on lower floors
  • Use fans, damp clothing and take cold baths frequently
  • Avoid going out in the sun between 12 noon and 3 p.m.
  • Visit at-risk adults at least twice a day and watch for heat exhaustion or heatstroke
  • Schedule strenuous jobs to cooler times of day and increase frequency and length of rest breaks for outdoor activities

  • Plan IEC activities as per the heatwave alert issued by IMD
  • Disseminate IMD heat-wave warnings to health facilities and vulnerable populations
  • Allow mass gathering events only if no severe heat wave warning is issued
  • Keep night shelters open through the day for migratory/vulnerable populations during a heat alert
  • Expand potable water access at religious places and transit stations; hand out water pouches in high-risk areas during a heat alert
  • Avoid scheduling mass gatherings on days with active heatwave warnings and high humidity
  • No open-air classes
  • Open cooling centres and provide transport for the most vulnerable
  • Tie the timing of interventions to the warning levels used; give educational messages early
  • Encourage workers to drink about one cup of cool water every 15–20 minutes
  • Alternate work and rest periods, with rest in a cool area
  • Ensure availability of coolers/fans at the work place
  • No worker should be allowed to work in direct exposure of sunlight
  • Slow down the pace of work, especially if workers feel even slight heat strain
  • Reschedule rest timings to avoid work in high temperatures; where not possible, assign a two-person crew
  • Avoid heat exposure; wear lightweight, light-coloured, loose cotton clothes; cover the head
  • Factories and mines: slow the pace of work in extreme heat, use two-person crews where continuous work is unavoidable, provide rest areas, ventilation and cooling
  • Ensure adequate drinking water, rest areas and cooling of work places
  • Stay indoors as much as possible; try to remain on lower floors
  • Use fans, damp clothing and take cold baths frequently
  • Avoid going out in the sun between 12 noon and 3 p.m.
  • Visit at-risk adults at least twice a day and watch for heat exhaustion or heatstroke
  • Schedule strenuous jobs to cooler times of day and increase frequency and length of rest breaks for outdoor activities

  • Avoid alcohol, tea, coffee and carbonated soft drinks; take a cold bath frequently
  • Drink water often, even if not thirsty
  • DISCOMs to generate awareness about running AC at 25 °C
  • Re-schedule load shedding
  • Record ward-wise heat stroke cases, cause of death, daily mortality and daily heat-related hospital admissions
  • Increase ASHA/ANM/MPHW outreach in at-risk villages during a heat alert, if feasible
  • Plan IEC activities as per the heatwave alert issued by IMD
  • Disseminate IMD heat-wave warnings to health facilities and vulnerable populations
  • Allow mass gathering events only if no severe heat wave warning is issued
  • Keep night shelters open through the day for migratory/vulnerable populations during a heat alert
  • Expand potable water access at religious places and transit stations; hand out water pouches in high-risk areas during a heat alert
  • Avoid scheduling mass gatherings on days with active heatwave warnings and high humidity
  • No open-air classes
  • Open cooling centres and provide transport for the most vulnerable
  • Tie the timing of interventions to the warning levels used; give educational messages early
  • Encourage workers to drink about one cup of cool water every 15–20 minutes
  • Alternate work and rest periods, with rest in a cool area
  • Ensure availability of coolers/fans at the work place
  • No worker should be allowed to work in direct exposure of sunlight
  • Slow down the pace of work, especially if workers feel even slight heat strain
  • Reschedule rest timings to avoid work in high temperatures; where not possible, assign a two-person crew
  • Avoid heat exposure; wear lightweight, light-coloured, loose cotton clothes; cover the head
  • Factories and mines: slow the pace of work in extreme heat, use two-person crews where continuous work is unavoidable, provide rest areas, ventilation and cooling
  • Ensure adequate drinking water, rest areas and cooling of work places
  • Stay indoors as much as possible; try to remain on lower floors
  • Use fans, damp clothing and take cold baths frequently
  • Avoid going out in the sun between 12 noon and 3 p.m.
  • Visit at-risk adults at least twice a day and watch for heat exhaustion or heatstroke
  • Schedule strenuous jobs to cooler times of day and increase frequency and length of rest breaks for outdoor activities

  • Ensure adequate drinking water, rest areas and cooling of work places
  • Stay indoors as much as possible; try to remain on lower floors
  • Use fans, damp clothing and take cold baths frequently
  • Avoid going out in the sun between 12 noon and 3 p.m.
  • Visit at-risk adults at least twice a day and watch for heat exhaustion or heatstroke
  • Schedule strenuous jobs to cooler times of day and increase frequency and length of rest breaks for outdoor activities

  2. Record ward-wise heat stroke cases, cause of death, daily mortality and daily heat-related hospital admissions (health)
    Precedent from Ahmedabad's Heat Action Plan (2019 update), not Delhi policy
    Recording ward wise heat stroke cases, proper cause of death and monitoring daily mortality as well as daily hospital admission due to heat-related causes.DELHI_HAP_2025, p.72

    confirm that night shelters stay open all day for migratory population during a heat alert.DELHI_HAP_2025, p.54
  5. Encourage workers to drink about one cup of cool water every 15–20 minutes (municipal)
    Encourage workers to drink plenty of water – about one cup of cool water every 15 to 20 minutes, even if they are not thirstyWHO_WMO_HEATWAVES_HEALTH_2015, p.72
  6. No worker should be allowed to work in direct exposure of sunlight (public)
    No worker should be allowed to work in the direct exposure of sunlight.DELHI_HAP_2025, p.156
  7. Avoid going out in the sun between 12 noon and 3 p.m. (public)
    Avoid going out in the sun, especially between 12.00 noon and 3.00 p.m.IMD_HW_FAQ, p.10
  8. Avoid heat exposure; wear lightweight, light-coloured, loose cotton clothes; cover the head (public)

  • Disseminate IMD heat-wave warnings to health facilities and vulnerable populations
  • Allow mass gathering events only if no severe heat wave warning is issued
  • Expand potable water access at religious places and transit stations; hand out water pouches in high-risk areas during a heat alert
  • Avoid scheduling mass gatherings on days with active heatwave warnings and high humidity
  • No open-air classes
  • Open cooling centres and provide transport for the most vulnerable
  • Tie the timing of interventions to the warning levels used; give educational messages early
  • Alternate work and rest periods, with rest in a cool area
  • Ensure availability of coolers/fans at the work place
  • Slow down the pace of work, especially if workers feel even slight heat strain
  • Reschedule rest timings to avoid work in high temperatures; where not possible, assign a two-person crew
  • Factories and mines: slow the pace of work in extreme heat, use two-person crews where continuous work is unavoidable, provide rest areas, ventilation and cooling
  • Ensure adequate drinking water, rest areas and cooling of work places
  • Stay indoors as much as possible; try to remain on lower floors
  • Use fans, damp clothing and take cold baths frequently
  • Visit at-risk adults at least twice a day and watch for heat exhaustion or heatstroke
  • Schedule strenuous jobs to cooler times of day and increase frequency and length of rest breaks for outdoor activities

Wed 18 May 202242.139.8+2.324%GREEN

Highest level: YELLOW IMD-style, adjusted for area-average data; not an official IMD warning

Summary

The highest alert is YELLOW on 14 and 15 May. Watch for heat exposure and stay hydrated.

Day by day

  • Saturday 14 May: maximum 42.5 °C, YELLOW alert.
  • Sunday 15 May: maximum 42.4 °C, YELLOW alert.
  • Monday 16 May: maximum 42.4 °C, GREEN alert.
  • Tuesday 17 May: maximum 42.1 °C, GREEN alert.
  • Wednesday 18 May: maximum 42.1 °C, GREEN alert.

Humid heat

Humid heat stress is expected on 14 May from our physical WBGT forecast, not an IMD category.

Highlighted actions

  1. DISCOMs to generate awareness about running AC at 25 °C (energy)
    Precedent from Ahmedabad's Heat Action Plan (2019 update), not Delhi policy
    Awareness generation to run the AC at 25degree centigrade,DELHI_HAP_2025, p.53
  2. Record ward-wise heat stroke cases, cause of death, daily mortality and daily heat-related hospital admissions (health)
    Precedent from Ahmedabad's Heat Action Plan (2019 update), not Delhi policy
    Recording ward wise heat stroke cases, proper cause of death and monitoring daily mortality as well as daily hospital admission due to heat-related causes.DELHI_HAP_2025, p.72
  3. Disseminate IMD heat-wave warnings to health facilities and vulnerable populations (health)
    Dissemination of early warning: of heat waves issued by the India Meteorological Department (IMD) daily after 1600 hours IST with a forecast for next four days should be disseminated to health facilities and vulnerable populations.NCDC_ADVISORY_STATES_2025, p.2
  4. Keep night shelters open through the day for migratory/vulnerable populations during a heat alert (municipal)
    confirm that night shelters stay open all day for migratory population during a heat alert.DELHI_HAP_2025, p.54
  5. Encourage workers to drink about one cup of cool water every 15–20 minutes (municipal)
    Encourage workers to drink plenty of water – about one cup of cool water every 15 to 20 minutes, even if they are not thirstyWHO_WMO_HEATWAVES_HEALTH_2015, p.72
  6. No worker should be allowed to work in direct exposure of sunlight (public)
    No worker should be allowed to work in the direct exposure of sunlight.DELHI_HAP_2025, p.156
  7. Avoid heat exposure; wear lightweight, light-coloured, loose cotton clothes; cover the head (public)
    (a) Avoid heat exposure. (b) Wear lightweight, light-coloured, loose, cotton clothes. (c) Cover your head: Use a cloth, hat or umbrellaIMD_HW_FAQ, p.8
  8. Avoid going out in the sun between 12 noon and 3 p.m. (public)
    Avoid going out in the sun, especially between 12.00 noon and 3.00 p.m.IMD_HW_FAQ, p.10
More actions (19, not rated)
  • Re-schedule load shedding
  • Increase ASHA/ANM/MPHW outreach in at-risk villages during a heat alert, if feasible
  • Plan IEC activities as per the heatwave alert issued by IMD
  • Allow mass gathering events only if no severe heat wave warning is issued
  • Expand potable water access at religious places and transit stations; hand out water pouches in high-risk areas during a heat alert
  • Avoid scheduling mass gatherings on days with active heatwave warnings and high humidity
  • No open-air classes
  • Open cooling centres and provide transport for the most vulnerable
  • Tie the timing of interventions to the warning levels used; give educational messages early
  • Alternate work and rest periods, with rest in a cool area
  • Ensure availability of coolers/fans at the work place
  • Slow down the pace of work, especially if workers feel even slight heat strain
  • Reschedule rest timings to avoid work in high temperatures; where not possible, assign a two-person crew
  • Factories and mines: slow the pace of work in extreme heat, use two-person crews where continuous work is unavoidable, provide rest areas, ventilation and cooling
  • Ensure adequate drinking water, rest areas and cooling of work places
  • Stay indoors as much as possible; try to remain on lower floors
  • Use fans, damp clothing and take cold baths frequently
  • Visit at-risk adults at least twice a day and watch for heat exhaustion or heatstroke
  • Schedule strenuous jobs to cooler times of day and increase frequency and length of rest breaks for outdoor activities

  • Schedule strenuous jobs to cooler times of day and increase frequency and length of rest breaks for outdoor activities

Highlighted actions

  1. Issue heat alerts via WhatsApp and SMS using telecom mobile databases (public)
    Precedent from Ahmedabad's Heat Action Plan (2019 update), not Delhi policy
    Issues heat alerts through WhatsApp and SMS platforms utilizing the centralized mobile databases of private sector telecom companies.DELHI_HAP_2025, p.80
  2. Send direct heat-alert messages to private medical practitioners, public hospitals and UHCs (health)
    Precedent from Ahmedabad's Heat Action Plan (2019 update), not Delhi policy
    Sends direct heat alert messages to private medical practitioners, public hospitals and UHCs.DELHI_HAP_2025, p.80
  3. Activate cooling centres (temples, public buildings, malls, temporary night shelters) during a heat alert (municipal)
    Precedent from Ahmedabad's Heat Action Plan (2019 update), not Delhi policy
    Activates “cooling centres,” such as temples, public buildings, malls, temporary night shelters, etc., during a heat alert.DELHI_HAP_2025, p.79
  4. DISCOMs to take special care of power supply to hospitals, dispensaries and clinics (energy)
    Precedent from Ahmedabad's Heat Action Plan (2019 update), not Delhi policy
    Special care should be taken for power supply in Hospitals, Dispensaries and Clinics.DELHI_HAP_2025, p.53
  5. Ensure adequate clean drinking water at the work place (public)
    Precedent from Ahmedabad's Heat Action Plan (2019 update), not Delhi policy
    Ensure adequate clean drinking water availability at the work place.DELHI_HAP_2025, p.155
  6. Send SMS warnings to residents of vulnerable areas during a heat alert (health)
    Precedent from Ahmedabad's Heat Action Plan (2019 update), not Delhi policy
    During a heat alert, disseminate SMS text messages to warn residents in the vulnerable areas.DELHI_HAP_2025, p.81
  7. Increase distribution of fresh drinking water to the public (water points / piau) (municipal)
    Precedent from Ahmedabad's Heat Action Plan (2019 update), not Delhi policy
    Increase efforts to distribute fresh drinking water to the public by openingDELHI_HAP_2025, p.54
  8. Prioritise uninterrupted power to critical facilities (hospitals, dispensaries/UHCs) (energy)
    Precedent from Ahmedabad's Heat Action Plan (2019 update), not Delhi policy
    Inform power supply companies to prioritize maintaining power to critical facilities (such as Hospitals and dispensaries).DELHI_HAP_2025, p.54
